All cited branches verified in source: b2b short-circuit packages/db/src/pricing.ts:95-102, floor derivation :112-113 and :123-124, nextTier.remainingNano saturation :133-135, `row.current_tier ?? 0` null-tier top row :106.

<a id="commerce-db--webhook-recovery-path-untested"></a>
#### `webhook-recovery-path-untested` — Missed-webhook reconciliation lookups are untested

**MEDIUM** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/db/src/checkouts.ts:86`

*Not covered today.* getCheckoutByProviderPayment (checkouts.ts:108) is NOT untested — it is exercised end-to-end against a live migrated database by the Platega webhook test, including its (provider, provider_payment_id) filter and the not-found branch that would throw. The genuinely uncovered function is listPendingCheckoutsForReconcile (checkouts.ts:86): its min/max age window, status='pending' and provider_payment_id IS NOT NULL filters have no test, and its only consumer apps/worker/src/platega-reconcile.service.ts:53 has no test file either. The 'money paid but never credited' risk stands for the reconcile lister only.

*Production risk.* This is the recovery path for money the customer actually paid but whose webhook never arrived. If the age window or the status filter regresses, those checkouts are never re-verified and the customer is silently never credited — the worst possible support outcome, and invisible in dashboards because the payment row never exists.

*Suggested test.* Integration test seeding four checkout_sessions: too young, in-window with provider_payment_id, in-window without one, and too old. Assert listPendingCheckoutsForReconcile returns exactly the second, ordered by created_at, respecting limit. Assert getCheckoutByProviderPayment resolves by (provider, providerPaymentId) and returns null for a matching id under the wrong provider.

<a id="commerce-db--window-close-downgrade-untested"></a>
#### `window-close-downgrade-untested` — Irreversible 30-day retention downgrade is untested, including its incomplete-data guard

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/db/src/pricing.ts:446`

*Not covered today.* packages/db/src/pricing.ts:446 closeElapsedTierWindows is DEAD CODE — nothing in the repo calls it. The production 30-day downgrade actually runs in apps/worker/src/pricing-worker.service.ts:149 (a private method of the same name, called at :97), which is a separate implementation: it gates on syncedUserIds from the current cycle, NOT on the puc.updated_at >= tier_window_start + interval '30 days' guard the claim describes (that guard exists only in the unused db copy). So the cited file carries no production risk (low). The real, still-untested gap is the worker's own copy — apps/worker has only config.test.ts and email-template.test.ts, so the live held-vs-not decision, downgrade reset and SKIP LOCKED loop are genuinely untested (high, but at a different path).

*Production risk.* A customer who met their hold gets downgraded anyway and pays 5-20 percentage points more on every subsequent request, with cumulative progress reset — commercially irreversible without manual intervention. Conversely a broken guard closes windows from a half-ingested ledger page, mass-downgrading customers after any transient engine outage. The SKIP LOCKED for(;;) loop also has no test proving it terminates rather than spinning against a row it cannot lock.

*Suggested test.* Integration test in packages/db. Seed a tier-2 (pro) profile with tier_window_start 31 days ago and pricing_usage_events summing exactly holdNano-1 → assert current_tier drops to 1, cumulative_topup_nano equals builder's threshold, tier_window_start equals the old window end, and a job with reason 'b2c_window_downgrade' exists. Repeat with exactly holdNano → assert tier retained and the window rolled forward. Third case: same expired window but pricing_usage_cursors.updated_at left at '-infinity' → assert the function returns 0 and touches nothing. Fourth: an event dated after the cutoff → assert refreshCurrentTierWindowSpend carried it into the new window's tier_window_spent_nano.

<a id="commerce-db--oauth-state-single-use"></a>
#### `oauth-state-single-use` — OAuth state transaction replay/expiry/provider-binding never tested

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/db/src/oauth.ts:46`

*Not covered today.* consumeOAuthTransaction's replay/expiry/provider-binding branches are indeed untested (only the happy path runs). But the stated production risk — 'a leaked callback URL becomes a replayable sign-in' — is guarded upstream: apps/api/src/auth.service.ts:265 rejects the callback unless the state also arrives in the apitoken_oauth_state cookie (double-submit, read at apps/api/src/auth.controller.ts:269), so a leaked URL alone cannot reach consumeOAuthTransaction. The residual untested risk is narrower: same-browser replay of a consumed/expired state re-applying inviteTokenHash or referralCode.

*Production risk.* Losing single-use enforcement on the OAuth state turns a leaked callback URL into a replayable sign-in, and the transaction also carries inviteTokenHash and referralCode — so a replay could re-apply a business invite or a partner discount floor to an attacker-chosen account.

*Suggested test.* Integration test in packages/db: createOAuthTransaction, consume once → row returned; consume again with the same stateHash → null; consume with the correct hash but the other provider → null; create one with expiresAt in the past → null. Assert consumed_at is set on the first consumption only.

<a id="commerce-db--integration-suites-silently-skip"></a>
#### `integration-suites-silently-skip` — All six integration suites no-op to green when TEST_DATABASE_URL is unset, and nothing asserts they ran

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/db/src/pricing-free-first.integration.test.ts:11`

*Not covered today.* The describe.runIf gating and the missing 'tests actually ran' assertion are real. Two supporting details are wrong and one failure mode is already guarded. (a) 'no vitest config in the repo' is false — apps/web/vitest.config.ts exists. (b) The local skip run is 24 passed / 26 skipped, not ~15. (c) The 'disposable-PG helper hands back an empty DSN' scenario is refuted: deploy/watchdog.sh:322 runs the migration with the same DSN before the tests, and packages/db/src/migrate.ts:51 throws 'DATABASE_URL is required' on an empty value, so an empty DSN reddens the gate before any test runs. The surviving real gap is narrower: a rename/refactor of the TEST_DATABASE_URL env name would silently skip every DB suite with a green status.

*Production risk.* If the disposable-PG helper ever hands back an empty DSN, or a refactor moves the env var name, the entire commerce database test suite evaporates into a green deploy/tests status and the watchdog ships the SHA. The gate would be attesting to coverage that did not run.

*Suggested test.* Add a guard test in packages/db that runs unconditionally: when process.env.CI or a dedicated REQUIRE_DB_TESTS flag is set, fail loudly if TEST_DATABASE_URL is missing. Set that flag in deploy/watchdog.sh alongside TEST_DATABASE_URL, and assert in deploy/watchdog-lib.test.sh (or a new gate assertion) that the run reported a minimum expected test count.

<a id="commerce-db--schema-migration-drift"></a>
#### `schema-migration-drift` — No check that schema.ts still matches the applied migrations

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/db/src/schema.ts:1`

*Not covered today.* There is genuinely no drizzle-kit check / snapshot diff / drift test. But 'All runtime queries in packages/db are raw SQL, so nothing at build or test time ever reads schema.ts against the database' is false: packages/db/src/sales-feed.ts:3 imports the payments, pricingUsageEvents and referralAttributions table objects from schema.ts and builds real drizzle queries from them, and packages/db/src/sales-feed.integration.test.ts:67,90 executes those queries against the freshly migrated disposable database — so column-level drift on the schema.ts definitions those feeds touch does fail the gate. The uncovered surface is the remaining tables, and the risk still requires a human to run db:generate and commit the emitted migration unreviewed.

*Production risk.* schema.ts drifts silently (it is only used as generator input). The next db:generate then emits a migration containing accumulated unintended diffs — potentially destructive contract changes — which the append-only manifest gate will happily accept because it is a new file, and the watchdog applies it to production after backup but without human review.

*Suggested test.* Add a packages/db test that runs drizzle-kit generate into a temp dir against the disposable PG schema (or runs `drizzle-kit check`) and fails if any statement would be produced, i.e. schema.ts is exactly represented by the committed migrations. Run it inside the existing pnpm -r test so it lands in the gate.

<a id="commerce-db--credit-and-email-retry-paths"></a>
#### `credit-and-email-retry-paths` — Retry/stale-recovery on the money and auth queues is untested

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/db/src/credits.ts:82`

*Not covered today.* retryCredit (credits.ts:82), recoverStaleCredits (credits.ts:100), retryEmail (email.ts:190) and recoverStaleEmails (email.ts:200) are confirmed to appear in no test file, and the lease-guard asymmetry is real as described. But the stated money consequence is refuted: a double engine credit from a reassigned lease cannot corrupt the accrual bookkeeping, because pricing_credit_accruals is keyed on credit_id with `ON CONFLICT (credit_id) DO NOTHING` and the eligible CTE requires `pca.credit_id IS NULL` (pricing.ts:713-726) — one engine_credits row accrues at most once regardless of how many times a worker processes it. Also note the surrounding happy paths ARE covered: exactly-once claiming and lease-guarded confirm for credits, and claim/confirm plus the terminal 'failed' state for email.

*Production risk.* recoverStaleCredits can reassign a lease that a still-live worker holds; the old worker's confirmCredit then fails silently (returns false) while the new worker credits the engine again — the ref makes the engine call idempotent, but the commerce-side accrual bookkeeping that reconcileTopupTier reads is driven off engine_credits.status. A wrong terminal threshold in retryEmail permanently drops verification/reset mail, locking users out.

*Suggested test.* Integration tests in packages/db: claim a credit, call retryCredit with a WRONG lease token → assert false and no mutation; with the right token → assert status='retry' and next_attempt_at ≈ now + 2^attempts capped at 3600. Backdate locked_at and call recoverStaleCredits → assert 1 and that the original lease token can no longer confirm. Mirror for email: attempts=9 → 'pending', attempts=10 → 'failed'.

### 8.10 `payments-worker` — Payment providers, engine-client, worker

This domain is the money ingress and egress of the commercial layer: three provider adapters (Cryptomus crypto invoices, Platega RUB/SBP, a dormant DigiSeller), the typed HTTP client that talks to the Rust engine Control API, and four NestJS background loops that deliver paid credits, send auth email, reconcile missed Platega callbacks, and recompute B2C discount tiers. Coverage is sharply bimodal. The pure adapter and client logic is genuinely well tested — 15 provider tests plus 12 engine-client tests pin exact FX arithmetic, bigint money fidelity, status-mapping tables, and secret-leak invariants, and apps/api/src/checkout.service.test.ts:135-198 exercises the Cryptomus and Platega webhook paths end-to-end against a real PostgreSQL. The four worker services are the opposite: apps/worker ships exactly two test files (config.test.ts, email-template.test.ts) covering 185 LOC of env validation and HTML rendering, while ~730 LOC of transactional money logic across credit-worker, pricing-worker, platega-reconcile and email-worker has zero tests of any kind — no unit, no integration, no shell e2e. Nine of the DB helpers those loops depend on (retryCredit, recoverStaleCredits, claimNextPricingJob, confirmPricingJob, retryPricingJob, recoverStalePricingJobs, listPendingCheckoutsForReconcile) are not referenced by a single test file anywhere in the repo. The biggest single quality defect is that the one Cryptomus signature test builds its fixture with the same serializer the implementation uses, so it can validate the hash construction but is structurally incapable of catching the PHP-vs-JS serialization mismatch that would silently reject every legitimate paid webhook.

**Working well here:**

- **Money passes through the engine client as exact integers, proven with a value JS number cannot hold** — `packages/engine-client/src/index.test.ts:7-24` — It asserts 9_007_199_254_740_993_123n serializes to the string "9007199254740993123" on the wire AND that the engine's JSON integer response comes back as the same exact string. Any regression to JSON.parse or Number() silently corrupts the low digits and this test fails immediately. Repeated deliberately for the ledger cursor (:189-190) and issueKey spend limits (:126-131). This is the single most valuable test in the domain.
- **Control-key and API-key secret hygiene pinned negatively** — `packages/engine-client/src/index.test.ts:57-69 and :141-143` — One test asserts x-api-key is absent on the unauthenticated /health request; another asserts the disable-key request body never contains the literal sk-pool-secret returned by the issue call. Both are negative assertions on secret propagation, which is exactly the class of bug code review misses and which CLAUDE.md calls out as an invariant.
- **Platega FX behaviour asserted on effects, not just shapes** — `packages/payments/src/platega.test.ts:104-130` — The crypto-method test threads a rateCalled boolean through the fetch stub and asserts it stayed false, proving the USD path never contacts Rapira, alongside the exact posted paymentDetails. Its sibling at :78-102 pins ceil(25*100*1.03)=2575 exactly. These would catch a real regression in what the customer is charged, not merely that a request was made.
- **Cryptomus status policy including the two states that lose money in opposite directions** — `packages/payments/src/cryptomus.test.ts:90-102` — It loops in-progress and refund-in-flight states asserting they never become "paid", then separates non-final wrong_amount (pending, customer can still top up the invoice) from final wrong_amount (canceled). That final-underpayment case is the difference between crediting an underpayment and leaving a checkout stuck pending forever, and it is asserted explicitly rather than assumed.
- **The core commercial invariant — credit the recorded USD, never the collected RUB — asserted on a real database row** — `apps/api/src/checkout.service.test.ts:135-178` — The test drives create -> RUB charge of 3700 -> CONFIRMED webhook -> and then reads engine_credits from live PostgreSQL, asserting exactly [{amount_nano: "37000000000"}]. It asserts the persisted financial effect rather than a return value, and it is the only place the FX boundary is proven not to leak into the engine credit.
- **Platega webhook header authentication has a negative test** — `apps/api/src/checkout.service.test.ts:180-198` — Platega callbacks carry no HMAC — the X-Secret/X-MerchantId comparison at apps/api/src/checkout.service.ts:111 is the entire authentication boundary for that endpoint. A wrong-secret test exists and asserts the specific PlategaWebhookAuthError message, so deleting or inverting that check fails the gate. Given that a forged CONFIRMED callback would mint free balance, having this covered is the right priority.
- **Provider TLS policy fully branch-covered** — `apps/worker/src/config.test.ts:29-49` — Three deepEqual assertions cover STARTTLS-required-in-production, implicit TLS on 465, and the relaxed local case. Small surface, security-relevant, and asserted as whole objects so an added or dropped tls option is caught.

<a id="payments-worker--pricing-worker-zero-tests"></a>
#### `pricing-worker-zero-tests` — The entire B2C tier engine (window close, downgrade, refund reversal) has no test of any kind

**CRITICAL** · verdict `CONFIRMED` · `apps/worker/src/pricing-worker.service.ts:149`

*Not covered today.* closeElapsedTierWindows (:149-244) and reverseRefundedTopups (:246-357) are ~210 lines of hand-written transactional SQL that decide every B2C customer's price multiplier, and no test file in the repo references PricingWorkerService. The specific untested decisions: the hold-vs-downgrade comparison at :193, the cumulative_topup_nano rewrite to nextRule.thresholdNano on downgrade at :196-198 (which permanently rewrites a customer's lifetime top-up total), the webhook_events synthetic idempotency guard at :286-301, and the FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED + ROLLBACK paths in both loops.

*Production risk.* Direct, silent, bidirectional revenue error on every paying customer. A wrong comparison at :193 either downgrades a customer who met their hold (they are overcharged, and the ledger will show it as legitimate) or retains a tier they lost (the business sells Anthropic quota at 20% of list to someone entitled to 40%). The refund-reversal guard at :286 is the only thing stopping a refund from being subtracted from cumulative_topup_nano on every poll cycle — if it regresses, a single refund walks a customer's tier down to zero over successive minutes. Neither failure produces an error or an alert; it just changes what everyone is billed.

*Suggested test.* Integration tests in apps/worker/src/pricing-worker.integration.test.ts against TEST_DATABASE_URL (the watchdog already provides it at deploy/watchdog.sh:328), driving the private methods via a thin exported helper or by instantiating the service with a stub EngineClient. Minimum table: (a) window elapsed and spend >= holdNano -> tier retained, new tier_window_start = old + 30d, no engine_pricing_jobs row; (b) window elapsed and spend one nano below holdNano -> tier drops exactly one step, multiplier_bp updated on both customer_profiles and engine_accounts, exactly one engine_pricing_jobs row with reason b2c_window_downgrade; (c) tier 0 -> tier_window_start becomes NULL and the loop terminates; (d) call reverseRefundedTopups twice over the same refunded payment and assert cumulative_topup_nano moved exactly once and webhook_events holds one refund-reversal:<id> row; (e) two concurrent invocations against three eligible rows -> each row closed exactly once (SKIP LOCKED).

*Verified absent by.* Searched: `rg -n 'PricingWorkerService|closeElapsedTierWindows|reverseRefundedTopups|recoverStalePricingJobs|claimNextPricingJob|listPricingSyncTargets|getPricingUsageCursor' --glob '*.test.ts'` -> zero hits in any test file. apps/worker/package.json test script is `tsx --test src/*.test.ts` and the only matching files are apps/worker/src/config.test.ts and apps/worker/src/email-template.test.ts, so the whole service layer is outside the runner. Line refs check out: hold comparison at apps/worker/src/pricing-worker.service.ts:193, cumulative rewrite at :196-198, private B2C_TIER_RULES lookups at :182/:195/:306, reverseRefundedTopups at :246, webhook_events synthetic guard at :287-301. No bash/e2e substitute either: deploy/test-stage2-e2e.sh never touches commerce tier state (only `account topup` at :63). ADDITIONAL evidence that makes this worse, not better: packages/db/src/pricing.ts:446 contains a rival `closeElapsedTierWindows` that uses the canonical B2C_PRICING_TIERS (:485, :494) and an explicit cursor-freshness guard the worker copy lacks — it is exported from neither packages/db/src/index.ts nor imported anywhere (`rg 'closeElapsedTierWindows' -g '*.ts'` shows only pricing.ts and the worker's own private method), i.e. the better, canonical-table implementation is dead code and the untested hand-rolled copy is what runs in production. Severity critical stands.

<a id="payments-worker--credit-worker-delivery-zero-tests"></a>
#### `credit-worker-delivery-zero-tests` — The loop that converts a paid payment into engine balance is untested, including its retry and lease-loss branches

**HIGH** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `apps/worker/src/credit-worker.service.ts:50`

*Not covered today.* CreditWorkerService has no test of any kind (confirmed), so the sequencing of claim -> engine credit -> confirm/retry is unverified. But the stated failure modes are mostly not reachable: (a) retryCredit does have exponential backoff — packages/db/src/credits.ts:90 `delaySeconds = Math.min(3600, Math.max(5, 2 ** Math.min(attempts, 10)))` — so 'spins the job forever without backoff' is wrong; (b) there is deliberately no give-up path at all (credit-worker.service.ts:74-96 always reaches retryCredit), so 'loses a paid credit permanently' is not reachable — if retryCredit's lease-scoped UPDATE matches 0 rows the row stays 'processing' and recoverStaleCredits (credits.ts:100-107) reclaims it after 5 minutes, and that recovery is invoked on a 60s timer at credit-worker.service.ts:45-48; (c) the double-delivery hazard is not caused by the confirmed===false branch — creditAccount runs before confirmCredit on every iteration regardless of that branch — it is prevented by claimNextCredit exclusivity, which IS tested at packages/db/src/payments.integration.test.ts:137-143 (concurrent worker-a/worker-b claim, exactly one wins) plus engine ref idempotency. The genuine untested residue: the throw-on-retry-persistence-failure at :94 (escalates to the outer catch and only sleeps), and that retryCredit/recoverStaleCredits have no test at all (`rg 'retryCredit|recoverStaleCredits' --glob '*.test.ts'` -> zero hits).

*Production risk.* This is the last hop between a customer's money and their usable balance. If the lease-loss branch is wrong, two workers can both call engine.creditAccount and the paid credit is delivered twice (engine ref idempotency is the only remaining backstop). If retryCredit regresses, a transient engine blip either loses a paid credit permanently or spins the job forever without backoff. Both failures happen after the customer has been charged, so the customer-facing symptom is silent balance loss.

*Suggested test.* apps/worker/src/credit-worker.integration.test.ts against TEST_DATABASE_URL with a stub EngineClient. Assert: (a) engine throws a retryable EngineClientError -> job returns to pending with attempts+1 and a future next_attempt_at, lease released; (b) engine throws a non-retryable EngineClientError -> job is still claimable (the documented intent at :79) and last_error is recorded; (c) engine succeeds but another worker stole the lease -> confirmCredit returns false and no duplicate engine call is issued on the next claim; (d) recoverStaleCredits reclaims a job whose locked_at is older than the lease window and leaves a fresh one alone; (e) a stub engine that throws on the second call proves the same idempotencyRef is reused, not regenerated.

<a id="payments-worker--tier-rules-duplicated-not-pinned"></a>
#### `tier-rules-duplicated-not-pinned` — The worker keeps its own copy of the B2C pricing table with no test asserting it matches the canonical one

**HIGH** · verdict `CONFIRMED` · `apps/worker/src/pricing-worker.service.ts:25`

*Not covered today.* B2C_TIER_RULES at pricing-worker.service.ts:25-31 re-declares thresholds, hold amounts and multipliers that already exist as B2C_PRICING_TIERS in packages/contracts/src/index.ts:255-261 (spendThresholdNano/holdNano/multiplierBp). The values currently agree, but the worker imports tierForTopups from @claude-api/db (which reads the contracts table) at :304 while using its own table at :182, :195 and :306 in the same function. No test asserts the two tables are equal, and no test touches the worker's copy at all. The same pattern repeats with the ledger page size: pricing-worker.service.ts:118 and :123 hardcode 1000 while packages/db/src/pricing.ts:53 defines PRICING_LEDGER_PAGE_SIZE = 1000 as a module-private constant used at :396 to decide reachedStablePageEnd.

*Production risk.* reverseRefundedTopups at :304-306 computes topupTier from the contracts table and then indexes the worker's table for the multiplier. If a pricing change lands in contracts only — the natural place to change it, since PRICING.md and the web pricing page both read from there — every window close and every refund reversal writes the stale multiplier to customer_profiles, engine_accounts and the engine itself. Everyone is billed at the old discount with no error anywhere. The page-size drift is milder: it would make the cursor's stable-page-end/retention signal wrong.

*Suggested test.* Delete the duplicate and import B2C_PRICING_TIERS. Until then, a one-line unit test in apps/worker (node:test, so it runs under the existing `tsx --test src/*.test.ts` script) asserting B2C_TIER_RULES maps element-wise onto B2C_PRICING_TIERS for thresholdNano/holdNano/multiplierBp, plus an assertion that the worker's page size equals an exported PRICING_LEDGER_PAGE_SIZE.

*Verified absent by.* apps/worker/src/pricing-worker.service.ts:25-31 declares a private B2C_TIER_RULES; packages/contracts/src/index.ts:255-261 declares the canonical B2C_PRICING_TIERS. The mixed use inside one function is real: tierForTopups (which reads the contracts table via packages/db/src/pricing.ts:43-49) is called at pricing-worker.service.ts:304 and the result immediately indexes the worker's own table at :306; same mixing at :182/:195. `rg 'B2C_TIER_RULES' -g '*.ts'` matches only that one file, so no test touches it, and no test compares the two tables. Page-size drift confirmed: literal 1000 at pricing-worker.service.ts:118 and :123 vs module-private PRICING_LEDGER_PAGE_SIZE = 1000 at packages/db/src/pricing.ts:53 used for reachedStablePageEnd at :396. Worse than claimed: `rg 'B2C_PRICING_TIERS' -g '*.ts'` shows a THIRD copy of this logic — packages/db/src/pricing.ts:446-514 closeElapsedTierWindows using the canonical table at :485/:494 — which is exported from neither packages/db/src/index.ts (grep returns nothing) nor imported anywhere, i.e. dead. The live path is the untested private table; the canonical-table path is dead. Severity high stands.

<a id="payments-worker--cryptomus-signature-fixture-is-self-generated"></a>
#### `cryptomus-signature-fixture-is-self-generated` — The only Cryptomus signature test generates its fixture with the implementation's own serializer, so it cannot catch a wire-format divergence

**MEDIUM** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/payments/src/cryptomus.ts:123`

*Not covered today.* The test is NOT purely tautological and does catch the one wire-format divergence that actually matters. packages/payments/src/cryptomus.test.ts:47-55 builds the webhook body with `JSON.stringify({...unsigned, sign})` — which leaves the callback URL's slashes literal ('https://apitoken.sale/payment/complete' at :53) — while the expected signature at :139-141 is computed over the slash-ESCAPED form. An implementation that hashed the received bytes, or that dropped phpCompatibleJson (cryptomus.ts:231-233), fails this test. Tamper rejection is covered at :61. Two of the claimed 'untested divergence classes' are non-risks: (a) 'sign not last' cannot break verification because verifyWebhook copies the JSON.parse'd object and `delete unsigned.sign` (cryptomus.ts:121-122), leaving the remaining keys in their original insertion order; (b) whitespace/pretty-printing is exactly what re-serialization normalizes away — hashing raw bytes would be the fragile choice. The accurate narrower claim: no test feeds a payload with non-ASCII field values (PHP json_encode's default \uXXXX escaping vs JSON.stringify) or with a numeric literal that re-serializes differently (1.0 -> 1), no captured real-provider fixture exists (verified: no fixtures directory anywhere in the repo), and an absent/short `sign` is only rejected by the schema at cryptomus.ts:38 with no test. The 'total invisible outage' framing is also weakened by the fact that this integration is live in production against real Cryptomus payloads.

*Production risk.* Fail-closed, but the failure is total and invisible. If Cryptomus emits any payload whose canonical form differs from JSON.stringify(JSON.parse(body)), every legitimate paid webhook is rejected with "signature is invalid", and unlike Platega there is NO Cryptomus reconciliation poller in apps/worker — the customer has paid crypto, the invoice shows paid on Cryptomus's side, and the balance is never credited until an operator notices. The current test suite would stay green through that entire outage because it never feeds a byte sequence the implementation did not itself produce.

*Suggested test.* Add packages/payments/src/__fixtures__/cryptomus-webhook-*.json holding verbatim captured bodies (from Cryptomus's webhook testing endpoint, documented in CRYPTOMUS_INTEGRATION.md) together with the signature Cryptomus actually sent, and assert verifyWebhook accepts them as raw strings. Then add hostile-payload unit tests: sign omitted -> "invalid shape"; sign present but 32 hex chars of the wrong value -> "signature is invalid" (exercising safeHexEqual on equal length); a body containing a Cyrillic or emoji field value; a body where a numeric field is written as 1.0. Longer term the finding is that the signature should be computed over the received bytes with the sign value spliced out, not over a re-serialization — a test on the raw-bytes fixture would force that.

<a id="payments-worker--platega-reconcile-zero-tests"></a>
#### `platega-reconcile-zero-tests` — The sole safety net for undelivered Platega callbacks is untested and silently swallows non-retryable errors

**MEDIUM** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `apps/worker/src/platega-reconcile.service.ts:70`

*Not covered today.* PlategaReconcileService is genuinely untested (confirmed) and listPendingCheckoutsForReconcile has no test, but the claim overstates the exposure because both halves it orchestrates are covered: the provider's verifyPayment state mapping and payload->checkoutId extraction are pinned at packages/payments/src/platega.test.ts:143-170 (CONFIRMED->paid with checkoutId, CANCELED->canceled, CHARGEBACKED->refunded), and the idempotent apply it calls is covered at packages/db/src/payments.integration.test.ts:94-110 (dedup by providerEventId, never a second credit). The 'credits row.amountUsd against a transaction belonging to a different checkout' risk is weak: `row` is selected from our own checkout_sessions by provider_payment_id (packages/db/src/checkouts.ts:86-104) and both checkoutId and amountUsd come from that row, so the payload comparison at :74-77 is defence-in-depth, not the primary binding. The accurate narrower claim: the orchestration branches — pending short-circuit (:73), the payload-mismatch skip (:74-77), and the catch at :92-95 that silently returns for retryable PlategaError and merely logs everything else (so an amount-mismatch rejection from applyVerifiedCheckoutPaymentEvent is indistinguishable from a network blip) — plus the 2-day abandonment cutoff at :13, are untested.

*Production risk.* Platega callbacks are authenticated only by headers and delivered over the public internet; this poller is what recovers a payment whose callback was lost. If it regresses, paid RUB checkouts sit pending until the 2-day MAX_AGE_SECONDS cutoff at :13 and are then abandoned forever with no alert. Conversely, if the checkout matching at :74-77 regresses, the poller credits row.amountUsd against a transaction that belongs to a different checkout.

*Suggested test.* apps/worker/src/platega-reconcile.integration.test.ts against TEST_DATABASE_URL with a stubbed fetch for the Platega provider. Assert: (a) a pending checkout older than minAgeSeconds whose provider says CONFIRMED gets exactly one engine_credits row for the locally recorded USD; (b) running it twice produces duplicateEvent:true and still exactly one credit; (c) provider says PENDING -> nothing written; (d) provider returns a payload naming a different checkout -> no credit and the warning path taken; (e) provider returns HTTP 503 -> PlategaError.retryable causes a silent return and the checkout remains eligible next cycle; (f) a checkout older than MAX_AGE_SECONDS is not returned by listPendingCheckoutsForReconcile.

<a id="payments-worker--engine-client-retryable-classification-unpinned"></a>
#### `engine-client-retryable-classification-unpinned` — Only one of the engine client's five error classifications is asserted, and the classification drives whether paid credits are retried

**MEDIUM** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/engine-client/src/index.ts:320`

*Not covered today.* The coverage facts are correct — `rg 'retryable' --glob '*.test.ts'` across apps and packages returns exactly one hit, packages/engine-client/src/index.test.ts:36, and no test drives a 500, a 429, a throwing fetch, or the AbortController timeout at packages/engine-client/src/index.ts:284-285 (no test file references EngineClientError at all). But the stated production risk is refuted by how the flag is consumed: `rg '\.retryable|isRetryableEngineFailure' -g '*.ts'` shows only three consumers, and none of them is a give-up path. credit-worker.service.ts:76-80 uses !retryable purely to choose a log message — retryCredit is called on every error either way, so a misclassified 429 or socket timeout changes log text, not behaviour, and cannot 'take the give-up path for a paid customer's balance'. account.service.ts:410-413 only selects HTTP 503 vs 500 in account.controller.ts:121 and payments.controller.ts:50. The real residue is the timeout path: index.ts:284-285 sets the AbortController and index.ts:300-311 maps abort to 'engine request timed out'; that whole path is unexercised, so a regression that never sets the signal would hang the credit and pricing worker loops with no test failing.

*Production risk.* credit-worker.service.ts:76 and account.service.ts:412 branch on this flag. If a 429 or a socket timeout were misclassified as non-retryable, the credit worker would take the give-up path for a paid customer's balance; if a 400 were misclassified as retryable, a permanently-broken account mapping would spin forever consuming a lease. The timeout path is entirely unexercised, so a regression that never clears the AbortController or never sets the signal would hang the worker loop on a wedged engine connection with no test failing.

*Suggested test.* Extend packages/engine-client/src/index.test.ts with a table over [status/behaviour -> {message, status, retryable}] covering: 500 JSON error body, 429, 503 non-JSON, 404, a fetch impl that throws TypeError, and a fetch impl that never resolves against timeoutMs:5 with vi.useFakeTimers, asserting message "engine request timed out" and retryable true. Assert on the EngineClientError fields, not just that it throws.

<a id="payments-worker--engine-client-credit-ref-contract-drift"></a>
#### `engine-client-credit-ref-contract-drift` — The engine rejects positive credits without a provider-qualified ref and requires i64 amounts; the client validates neither and no test pins the contract

**MEDIUM** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/engine-client/src/index.ts:108`

*Not covered today.* Both cited source facts are accurate (crates/server/src/admin.rs:357-368 i64 parse, :383-389 provider-qualified ref for amount>0, helper qualified_topup_ref at :330-339 with no Rust unit test — no #[cfg(test)] exists in admin.rs and there is no crates/server/tests directory), and there is genuinely no cross-language contract test. But the 'unqualified ref reaches production' risk is largely theoretical because every existing call site's ref string is separately pinned by a test: payments (`cryptomus:<uuid>`) at packages/db/src/payments.integration.test.ts:91, welcome bonus (`signup-bonus:<userId>`) at apps/api/src/engine-provisioning.test.ts:19 and apps/api/src/auth.service.test.ts:253, admin credit (`admin-credit:<key>`) at apps/api/src/admin-operations.service.test.ts:116, and the provider/promo/admin prefix taxonomy at packages/db/src/pricing.unit.test.ts:8-15. All satisfy qualified_topup_ref. The i64 half is unreachable in production: checkout amounts are bounded by MAX_TOPUP_USD at apps/api/src/checkout.service.ts:32-35, and the bonus is a 4e9 constant. The accurate narrower claim: a NEW credit call site could ship an unqualified ref and only fail at runtime as a 400 that the credit worker retains for retry forever, and a Rust-side tightening of qualified_topup_ref would turn nothing red on the TypeScript side.

*Production risk.* A caller that passes an unqualified ref (a bare payment UUID, or a new code path that forgets the prefix) gets a 400 classified as non-retryable, which credit-worker.service.ts:76-80 deliberately retains for retry — so the paid credit never lands and the job cycles forever with no alert. An amount above i64::MAX serializes fine and is rejected identically. Symmetrically, if the Rust side tightens qualified_topup_ref, nothing in the TypeScript test suite goes red before the watchdog deploys it.

*Suggested test.* In packages/engine-client/src/index.test.ts assert creditAccount rejects locally (RangeError, before fetch) for a ref lacking a colon, a ref with whitespace on either side of the colon, and an amountNano above maxSignedI64 — mirroring admin.rs:333-339 qualified_topup_ref. Separately add a contract test that asserts the exact JSON body shapes the client emits for credit/issueKey/policy/pricing against fixtures generated from the Rust handlers' Deserialize structs, so `#[serde(deny_unknown_fields)]` on CreditReq (admin.rs:324) cannot be broken by an added client field.

<a id="payments-worker--debit-account-bonus-revocation-untested"></a>
#### `debit-account-bonus-revocation-untested` — debitAccount, the only path that removes real balance from a customer, has no test anywhere

**MEDIUM** · verdict `CONFIRMED` · `packages/engine-client/src/index.ts:124`

*Not covered today.* debitAccount (index.ts:124-134) negates the amount and posts it to the same /credit endpoint. Its sole caller is the welcome-bonus revocation at apps/api/src/admin-operations.service.ts:221. A repo-wide grep for `debitAccount` finds only the definition and that one call site — no test file references it. Nothing asserts the sign inversion (a missing minus would credit instead of debit), nothing asserts the amountNano <= 0n guard, and nothing asserts that ref reuse is what makes the operation idempotent. Note that admin.rs:384 only enforces the provider-qualified ref for positive amounts, so a negative credit with a malformed ref is accepted by the engine.

*Production risk.* A sign regression turns a bonus revocation into a bonus grant — an admin action intended to claw back abuse instead doubles it, and because the operation is idempotent-by-ref it succeeds cleanly. The service's replay path at admin-operations.service.ts:206-219 also short-circuits on a prior ledger row, so the second observable symptom would be a permanently wrong balance rather than an error.

*Suggested test.* In packages/engine-client/src/index.test.ts assert debitAccount(acct, 5_000_000_000n, "revoke:user-1") posts exactly {"amount_nano":"-5000000000","ref":"revoke:user-1"}, that a zero or negative amountNano throws RangeError before fetch, and that a response naming a different account raises "engine returned a response for a different account" (assertAccount at index.ts:314 is currently only exercised on the happy path).

*Verified absent by.* `rg 'debitAccount' -g '*.ts' --glob '!node_modules'` returns exactly two lines: the definition at packages/engine-client/src/index.ts:124 and the single call site at apps/api/src/admin-operations.service.ts:221. No test file references it. apps/api/src/admin-operations.service.test.ts (read in full, 195 lines) exercises dashboard/topups (:82), creditUser idempotency (:102), setUserStatus (:126) and resetTotp (:158) — revokeSignupBonus is absent, and its FakeAdminEngine at :174-195 records credits without ever asserting a sign. So nothing pins the `(-amountNano)` inversion at index.ts:133, the amountNano <= 0n guard at :125, or the ref-reuse idempotency. The claim's supporting reading of the engine is also correct: crates/server/src/admin.rs:383-389 applies the provider-qualified-ref check only when nano > 0, so `bonus-revoke:<uuid>` (which happens to be qualified anyway) is unvalidated on the negative path. Severity medium stands — this is an operator-initiated path, not a per-request one.

<a id="payments-worker--payment-provider-no-timeouts"></a>
#### `payment-provider-no-timeouts` — Neither payment provider bounds its outbound HTTP calls, and Cryptomus network errors escape the adapter's own error type

**MEDIUM** · verdict `CONFIRMED` · `packages/payments/src/cryptomus.ts:163`

*Not covered today.* A grep for AbortController/AbortSignal/timeout across packages/payments/src returns nothing — neither CryptomusProvider.request (cryptomus.ts:161-188) nor PlategaProvider.request (platega.ts:220-254) nor rapiraUsdRubRate (platega.ts:196-218) sets a signal, in contrast with EngineClient.request which does (index.ts:284-285). Additionally the Cryptomus fetch at :163 is not wrapped in try/catch, so a DNS or TCP failure rejects with a raw TypeError rather than a CryptomusError; apps/api/src/payments.controller.ts:45 only maps CryptomusError/PlategaError to 502, so that case falls through to a 500. Platega does wrap its fetch (platega.ts:239-241) but that branch has no test either. No test in the package drives a rejecting or hanging fetch for any provider.

*Production risk.* A wedged provider connection pins the request indefinitely — inside the webhook handler that means a held Node request slot per stuck callback, and inside PlategaReconcileService.reconcileOne it means the whole reconcile loop stalls, since :59-62 awaits each row serially. The Cryptomus error-type gap additionally turns a transient network blip into a 500 (Cryptomus retries on non-2xx, so it recovers, but the signal is wrong and checkout.service.ts:68 permanently fails the checkout session for a transient blip during create).

*Suggested test.* Unit tests in cryptomus.test.ts and platega.test.ts with a fetch stub that (a) rejects with a TypeError — assert the thrown error is a CryptomusError/PlategaError with retryable true, and (b) returns a never-resolving promise under vi.useFakeTimers — assert the adapter aborts after its configured timeout. Both currently fail, which is the point: they encode the missing AbortController and the missing try/catch as requirements.

*Verified absent by.* `rg -n 'AbortController|AbortSignal|timeout|signal' packages/payments/src/` returns only two unrelated hits (a test title at platega.test.ts:132 and a doc comment at provider.ts:65) — no provider bounds any outbound call, in contrast with packages/engine-client/src/index.ts:284-285. The Cryptomus asymmetry is real: cryptomus.ts:163-171 awaits this.fetchImpl with no try/catch, so a DNS/TCP failure escapes as a raw TypeError, while platega.ts:234-242 and :196-201 do wrap theirs in PlategaError(retryable=true). apps/api/src/payments.controller.ts:45 (and :76, :95) map only CryptomusError/PlategaError to BadGateway, so the raw TypeError falls through. No test drives a rejecting or hanging fetch for any provider — packages/payments/src/platega.test.ts:172-185 is the closest and only covers a non-positive rate value, not a throwing fetch. The serial-await stall in apps/worker/src/platega-reconcile.service.ts:59-62 and the permanent failCheckoutSession on a transient create error at apps/api/src/checkout.service.ts:68 are both as described. Severity medium stands.

<a id="payments-worker--pricing-ledger-page-replay-untested"></a>
#### `pricing-ledger-page-replay-untested` — The ledger replay path the sync loop is explicitly designed around is never tested

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `apps/worker/src/pricing-worker.service.ts:112`

*Not covered today.* Correct that no test calls applyPricingLedgerPage twice with the same entries — packages/db/src/pricing-free-first.integration.test.ts (87 lines, read in full) has exactly three cases at :59, :69 and :79, none of which replays a page — and correct that free top-ups have no ON CONFLICT of their own, leaving the startCursor skip at packages/db/src/pricing.ts:345/:353 as the only guard against double-crediting free_balance_nano. The claim slightly overstates reachability: within applyPricingLedgerPage the cursor UPDATE (:397-402) is in the same transaction as the effects, and the worker's syncTarget re-reads the durable cursor from the DB each cycle (pricing-worker.service.ts:113-116), so a same-process replay cannot occur — the guard only matters for a concurrent second worker instance, where it works because the cursor is read (:341-345) AFTER the `FOR UPDATE` lock on customer_profiles (:330-333). The comment at pricing-worker.service.ts:114-116 explains the ack-replay case, which is a re-ack, not a re-apply. So this is an untested concurrency guard rather than a routinely-exercised path.

*Production risk.* A double-applied page inflates free_balance_nano, and free balance is spent before real money and earns no partner commission (packages/db/src/pricing.ts:31-39). So a replay bug hands customers free inference and simultaneously suppresses partner payouts — money out on both sides, with no error and no reconciliation job that would notice.

*Suggested test.* Add to packages/db/src/pricing-free-first.integration.test.ts: apply a page containing a free top-up and a charge, capture free_balance_nano and SUM(real_funded_nano), apply the identical page again, and assert both are byte-identical. Add a partial-overlap case (page two repeats the last entry of page one plus new entries) asserting only the new entries take effect. Pair it with a worker-level test that stubs acknowledgeLedger to throw after the first successful applyPricingLedgerPage and asserts the next cycle converges without double-counting.

<a id="payments-worker--email-worker-at-least-once-token-resend"></a>
#### `email-worker-at-least-once-token-resend` — The email outbox's retry semantics are untested at the service level, including double-send of a live single-use auth token

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `apps/worker/src/email-worker.service.ts:88`

*Not covered today.* The service-level gaps are real: EmailWorkerService is referenced by no test, `rg 'retryEmail|recoverStaleEmails' --glob '*.test.ts'` returns zero hits, the send-then-confirm sequence at apps/worker/src/email-worker.service.ts:88-95 will re-send the same decrypted token if confirmEmail fails, the fail-fast SIGTERM at :56-61 is unverified, and the decrypt-throw path at :106 (post key rotation) is untested — packages/db/src/email.integration.test.ts:80-94 covers only a payload rejected at claim time. But the headline production risk is refuted by the code: retryEmail is NOT unbounded — packages/db/src/email.ts:190-197 sets `terminal = job.attempts >= 10` and flips the row to status 'failed', with exponential backoff at :192. So 'an infinite retry that never surfaces as a failed job' cannot happen; after 10 attempts the outbox row is a visible failed job. The accurate narrower claim is the duplicate-token-send window plus the unverified liveness guard.

*Production risk.* Duplicate verification emails are a support annoyance; the real hazard is a key rotation or SMTP misconfiguration turning every claim into an infinite retry that never surfaces as a failed job, so nobody can sign up and the outbox silently grows. The SIGTERM self-kill at :60 is the only liveness guard and is unverified — if it regressed the worker would sit alive with a dead loop, which reads as healthy to systemd.

*Suggested test.* apps/worker/src/email-worker.integration.test.ts against TEST_DATABASE_URL with a stub transport. Assert: (a) sendMail resolves but confirmEmail throws -> job returns to pending with attempts+1 and the transport was called exactly once in that iteration; (b) sendMail rejects -> retryEmail records last_error and backs off; (c) attempts exceeding the max -> status failed, not an infinite loop; (d) decryptAuthToken throwing with a rotated key -> the job fails rather than spinning; (e) recoverStaleEmails reclaims a locked_at older than the lease and leaves a fresh lock alone.

<a id="payments-worker--digiseller-hmac-equality-never-exercised"></a>
#### `digiseller-hmac-equality-never-exercised` — The DigiSeller checkout-tracking HMAC test rejects on length, never on the signature comparison, and guards unreachable code

**LOW** · verdict `ADJUSTED` · `packages/payments/src/digiseller.ts:209`

*Not covered today.* The core point survives — the only negative tracking test, packages/payments/src/digiseller.test.ts:74-86, supplies checkout_sig="00" (1 byte vs a 32-byte HMAC), so it exits at the length comparison in digiseller.ts:219 and no test ever supplies an equal-length WRONG signature; an always-true comparison mutant would survive. But the claim's phrasing 'the timingSafeEqual call is never reached with equal-length inputs' is factually wrong: the positive test at digiseller.test.ts:29-72 builds a real full-length HMAC (:32-33) and asserts checkoutId comes back matching (:69), which reaches timingSafeEqual with equal-length, equal inputs. Also, verifyUniqueCode is not untested — it has a positive test at digiseller.test.ts:88+ — the untested part is that it hardcodes state:'paid' and providerEventId `${inv}:3` with no invoice-state check (digiseller.ts:126-129). Dormancy confirmed: the provider is constructed in apps/api/src/payments.module.ts:21 but `rg 'digiseller' apps/api/src` finds no route, and apps/api/src/checkout.service.ts:20 restricts PaymentProviderCode to cryptomus|platega.

*Production risk.* Low today precisely because the provider is dormant. It becomes high the moment DigiSeller is re-enabled: a forged query_string would bind someone else's paid invoice to an attacker-chosen checkoutId, and verifyUniqueCode's unconditional "paid" would credit a non-paid invoice. The test suite would report the provider as covered.

*Suggested test.* Change the tamper test to sign a different checkoutId with the same secret — a valid 32-byte HMAC over the wrong message — and assert checkoutId is null; add a case where checkout_sig is correct but checkout_id was swapped in the query string. Then either delete verifyUniqueCode and the DigiSeller registration, or add the missing invoice-state check plus a test that a non-paid unique-code lookup does not report state "paid".

<a id="payments-worker--worker-config-refinements-unasserted"></a>
#### `worker-config-refinements-unasserted` — None of the worker's production env guardrails has a negative test, including the canonical-origin lock on auth links

**LOW** · verdict `CONFIRMED` · `apps/worker/src/config.ts:38`

*Not covered today.* config.test.ts:22-27 asserts only that a valid production environment parses. The four superRefine branches at config.ts:29-53 have no rejection test: SMTP fields required when EMAIL_DELIVERY_MODE=smtp (:29), username/password paired (:32), SMTP mandatory in production (:35), and the PUBLIC_APP_BASE_URL lock to exactly https://apitoken.sale with empty username/password/path/query/hash (:38-53).

*Production risk.* The origin lock exists so a mis-set env var cannot mint verify-email and reset-password links pointing at a host the operator does not control — i.e. an account-takeover vector via emailed tokens. It is enforced only by that refinement, and nothing proves the refinement still rejects. The other three would surface as a crash-on-boot rather than silent damage, so they matter less.
